href="#cite_note-stmatthewscathedral.org-2">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup></td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label"><span class="nowrap">Major works</span></th><td class="infobox-data"><a href="/wiki/Gospel_of_Matthew" title="Gospel of Matthew">Gospel of Matthew</a></td></tr></tbody></table>
<p><b>Matthew the Apostle</b> (<b>Saint Matthew</b>)<sup id="cite_ref-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-3">&#91;a&#93;</a></sup> is named in the <a href="/wiki/New_Testament" title="New Testament">New Testament</a> as one of the <a href="/wiki/Twelve_Apostles" class="mw-redirect" title="Twelve Apostles">twelve apostles of Jesus</a>. According to Christian traditions, he was also one of the <a href="/wiki/Four_Evangelists" title="Four Evangelists">four Evangelists</a> as author of the <a href="/wiki/Gospel_of_Matthew" title="Gospel of Matthew">Gospel of Matthew</a>, and thus is also known as <b>Matthew the Evangelist</b>, a claim rejected by most biblical scholars, though the "traditional authorship still has its defenders."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAllison201027_4-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAllison201027-4">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The New Testament records that as a <a href="/wiki/Disciple_(Christianity)" title="Disciple (Christianity)">disciple</a>, he followed Jesus, and was one of the witnesses of the <a href="/wiki/Ascension_of_Jesus" title="Ascension of Jesus">Ascension of Jesus</a>. Later Church fathers such as <a href="/wiki/Irenaeus" title="Irenaeus">Irenaeus</a> and <a href="/wiki/Clement_of_Alexandria" title="Clement of Alexandria">Clement of Alexandria</a> claim that Matthew preached the Gospel to the Jewish community in <a href="/wiki/Judea" title="Judea">Judea</a>, before going to other countries.
</p>
<meta property="mw:PageProp/toc" />
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="In_the_New_Testament">In the New Testament</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Matthew_the_Apostle&amp;action=edit&amp;section=1" title="Edit section: In the New Testament">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:172px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Bodleian_Library_MS._Arm._d.13._Armenian_Gospels-0039-0.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/2d/Bodleian_Library_MS._Arm._d.13._Armenian_Gospels-0039-0.jpg/170px-Bodleian_Library_MS._Arm._d.13._Armenian_Gospels-0039-0.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="245" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/2d/Bodleian_Library_MS._Arm._d.13._Armenian_Gospels-0039-0.jpg/255px-Bodleian_Library_MS._Arm._d.13._Armenian_Gospels-0039-0.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/2d/Bodleian_Library_MS._Arm._d.13._Armenian_Gospels-0039-0.jpg/340px-Bodleian_Library_MS._Arm._d.13._Armenian_Gospels-0039-0.jpg 2x" data-file-width="710" data-file-height="1024" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Bodleian_Library_MS._Arm._d.13._Armenian_Gospels-0039-0.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Matthew in a painted miniature from a volume of Armenian Gospels dated 1609, held by the <a href="/wiki/Bodleian_Library" title="Bodleian Library">Bodleian Library</a></div></div></div>
<p>Matthew is mentioned in <a href="https://en.wikisource.org/wiki/Bible_(King_James)/Matthew#9:9" class="extiw" title="s:Bible (King James)/Matthew">Matthew 9:9</a> and <a href="https://en.wikisource.org/wiki/Bible_(King_James)/Matthew#10:3" class="extiw" title="s:Bible (King James)/Matthew">Matthew 10:3</a> as a <a href="/wiki/Tax_collector#Tax_collectors_in_the_Bible" title="Tax collector">tax collector</a> (<a href="/wiki/NIV" class="mw-redirect" title="NIV">NIV</a>) who, while sitting at the "receipt of custom" in <a href="/wiki/Capernaum" title="Capernaum">Capernaum</a>, was called to follow Jesus.<sup id="cite_ref-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-5">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup> He is also listed among <a href="/wiki/Twelve_Disciples" class="mw-redirect" title="Twelve Disciples">the twelve</a>, but without identification of his background, in Mark 3:18, Luke 6:15 and Acts 1:13.<sup id="cite_ref-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-6">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup> In passages parallel to Matthew 9:9, both Mark 2:14<sup id="cite_ref-7" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-7">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> and Luke 5:27<sup id="cite_ref-8" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-8">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup> describe Jesus's calling of the tax collector Levi, the son of Alphaeus, but Mark and Luke never explicitly equate this Levi with the Matthew named as one of the twelve apostles.
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Ministry">Ministry</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Matthew_the_Apostle&amp;action=edit&amp;section=2" title="Edit section: Ministry">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>The New Testament records that as a <a href="/wiki/Disciple_(Christianity)" title="Disciple (Christianity)">disciple</a>, he followed Jesus, and was one of the witnesses of the <a href="/wiki/Ascension_of_Jesus" title="Ascension of Jesus">Ascension of Jesus</a>. Afterwards, the disciples withdrew to an upper room (Acts 1:10–14)<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFreedman2001130–133,_201_9-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFreedman2001130–133,_201-9">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup>(traditionally the <a href="/wiki/Cenacle" title="Cenacle">Cenacle</a>) in <a href="/wiki/Early_centers_of_Christianity#Jerusalem" class="mw-redirect" title="Early centers of Christianity">Jerusalem</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEJacquier1911_10-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEJacquier1911-10">&#91;9&#93;</a></sup> The disciples remained in and about Jerusalem and proclaimed that Jesus was the promised Messiah.
</p><p>In the <a href="/wiki/Babylonian_Talmud" class="mw-redirect" title="Babylonian Talmud">Babylonian Talmud</a> (Sanhedrin 43a), "Mattai" is one of five disciples of "<a href="/wiki/Jeshu" class="mw-redirect" title="Jeshu">Jeshu</a>".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESchneemelcher200317_11-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESchneemelcher200317-11">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Later Church fathers such as <a href="/wiki/Irenaeus" title="Irenaeus">Irenaeus</a> (Against Heresies 3.1.1) and <a href="/wiki/Clement_of_Alexandria" title="Clement of Alexandria">Clement of Alexandria</a> claim that Matthew preached the Gospel to the Jewish community in Judea, before going to other countries. Ancient writers are not in agreement as to which other countries these are.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEJacquier1911_10-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEJacquier1911-10">&#91;9&#93;</a></sup> The Catholic Church and the Orthodox Church each hold the tradition that Matthew died as a <a href="/wiki/Martyr" title="Martyr">martyr</a> and the Babylonian Talmud appears to report his execution in Sanhedrin 43a<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELardner1838299_12-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELardner1838299-12">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBock2002164_13-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBock2002164-13">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup> although this was rejected by <a href="/wiki/Heracleon" title="Heracleon">Heracleon</a>, a <a href="/wiki/Gnosticism" title="Gnosticism">Gnostic Christian</a> viewed as a heretic, as early as the second century.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEOrr19152009_14-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEOrr19152009-14">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span id="Matthew.27s_Gospel"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Matthew's_Gospel">Matthew's Gospel</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Matthew_the_Apostle&amp;action=edit&amp;section=3" title="Edit section: Matthew&#039;s Gospel">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1033289096">.mw-parser-output .hatnote{font-style:italic}.mw-parser-output div.hatnote{padding-left:1.6em;margin-bottom:0.5em}.mw-parser-output .hatnote i{font-style:normal}.mw-parser-output .hatnote+link+.hatnote{margin-top:-0.5em}</style><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Gospel_of_Matthew" title="Gospel of Matthew">Gospel of Matthew</a></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">See also: <a href="/wiki/Gospel_of_Pseudo-Matthew" title="Gospel of Pseudo-Matthew">Gospel of Pseudo-Matthew</a></div>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:172px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Rembrandt_-_Evangelist_Matthew_and_the_Angel_-_WGA19119.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f1/Rembrandt_-_Evangelist_Matthew_and_the_Angel_-_WGA19119.jpg/170px-Rembrandt_-_Evangelist_Matthew_and_the_Angel_-_WGA19119.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="204" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f1/Rembrandt_-_Evangelist_Matthew_and_the_Angel_-_WGA19119.jpg/255px-Rembrandt_-_Evangelist_Matthew_and_the_Angel_-_WGA19119.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f1/Rembrandt_-_Evangelist_Matthew_and_the_Angel_-_WGA19119.jpg/340px-Rembrandt_-_Evangelist_Matthew_and_the_Angel_-_WGA19119.jpg 2x" data-file-width="804" data-file-height="966" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Rembrandt_-_Evangelist_Matthew_and_the_Angel_-_WGA19119.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div><i>Saint Matthew and the Angel</i> (1661) by <a href="/wiki/Rembrandt" title="Rembrandt">Rembrandt</a></div></div></div>
<p>The Gospel of Matthew is anonymous: the author is not named within the text, and the superscription "according to Matthew" was added some time in the second century.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHarrington19918_15-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarrington19918-15">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENolland200516_16-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENolland200516-16">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup> The tradition that the author was the disciple Matthew begins with the early Christian bishop <a href="/wiki/Papias_of_Hierapolis" title="Papias of Hierapolis">Papias of Hierapolis</a> (c. AD 60–163),<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMartin2012_17-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMartin2012-17">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup> who is cited by the Church historian <a href="/wiki/Eusebius" title="Eusebius">Eusebius</a> (AD 260–340), as follows: "Matthew collected the oracles (<i><a href="/wiki/Logia" title="Logia">logia</a></i>: sayings of or about Jesus) in the Hebrew language (<i>Hebraïdi dialektōi</i>), and each one interpreted (<i>hērmēneusen</i>&#160;– perhaps "translated") them as best he could."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETurner200815–16_18-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETurner200815–16-18">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Eusebius_19-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Eusebius-19">&#91;b&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBingham199864_20-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBingham199864-20">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On the surface, this has been taken to imply that Matthew's Gospel itself was written in Hebrew or Aramaic by the apostle Matthew and later translated into Greek, but nowhere does the author claim to have been an eyewitness to events, and Matthew's Greek "reveals none of the telltale marks of a translation".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHagner1986281_21-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHagner1986281-21">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHarrington19918_15-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarrington19918-15">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup> Scholars have put forward several theories to explain Papias: perhaps Matthew wrote two gospels, one, now lost, in Hebrew, the other our Greek version; or perhaps the <i>logia</i> was a collection of sayings rather than the gospel; or by <i>dialektōi</i> Papias may have meant that Matthew wrote in the Jewish style rather than in the Hebrew language.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETurner200815–16_18-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETurner200815–16-18">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup> The consensus is that Papias does not describe the Gospel of Matthew as we know it, and it is generally accepted that Matthew was written in Greek, not in Aramaic or Hebrew.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman199943_22-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman199943-22">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> Therefore, while the traditional authorship still has its defenders, the majority of mainstream Bible scholars rejects the Matthean authorship of the gospel.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMuddimanBarton201027_23-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMuddimanBarton201027-23">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAllison201027_4-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAllison201027-4">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Non-canonical_or_apocryphal_gospels">Non-canonical or apocryphal gospels</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Matthew_the_Apostle&amp;action=edit&amp;section=4" title="Edit section: Non-canonical or apocryphal gospels">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:172px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Matthaeus_San_Giovanni_in_Laterano_2006-09-07.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/80/Matthaeus_San_Giovanni_in_Laterano_2006-09-07.jpg/170px-Matthaeus_San_Giovanni_in_Laterano_2006-09-07.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="309" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/80/Matthaeus_San_Giovanni_in_Laterano_2006-09-07.jpg/255px-Matthaeus_San_Giovanni_in_Laterano_2006-09-07.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/80/Matthaeus_San_Giovanni_in_Laterano_2006-09-07.jpg/340px-Matthaeus_San_Giovanni_in_Laterano_2006-09-07.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1033" data-file-height="1878" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Matthaeus_San_Giovanni_in_Laterano_2006-09-07.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div><i>Saint Matthew</i> (1713–1715) by <a href="/wiki/Camillo_Rusconi" title="Camillo Rusconi">Camillo Rusconi</a>, <a href="/wiki/Archbasilica_of_St._John_Lateran" class="mw-redirect" title="Archbasilica of St. John Lateran">Archbasilica of St. John Lateran</a> in Rome</div></div></div>
<p>In the 3rd century, <a href="/wiki/Jewish%E2%80%93Christian_gospels" title="Jewish–Christian gospels">Jewish–Christian gospels</a> attributed to Matthew were used by Jewish–Christian groups such as the <a href="/wiki/Nazarene_(sect)" title="Nazarene (sect)">Nazarenes</a> and <a href="/wiki/Ebionites" title="Ebionites">Ebionites</a>. Fragments of these gospels survive in quotations by <a href="/wiki/Jerome" title="Jerome">Jerome</a>, <a href="/wiki/Epiphanius_of_Salamis" title="Epiphanius of Salamis">Epiphanius</a> and others. Most academic study follows the distinction of <a href="/wiki/Gospel_of_the_Nazarenes" title="Gospel of the Nazarenes">Gospel of the Nazarenes</a> (36 fragments), <a href="/wiki/Gospel_of_the_Ebionites" title="Gospel of the Ebionites">Gospel of the Ebionites</a> (7 fragments), and <a href="/wiki/Gospel_of_the_Hebrews" title="Gospel of the Hebrews">Gospel of the Hebrews</a> (7 fragments) found in <a href="/wiki/Schneemelcher" class="mw-redirect" title="Schneemelcher">Schneemelcher</a>'s <a href="/wiki/New_Testament_Apocrypha" class="mw-redirect" title="New Testament Apocrypha">New Testament Apocrypha</a>. Critical commentators generally regard these texts as having been composed in Greek and related to Greek Matthew.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEVielhauerStrecker2003542_24-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEVielhauerStrecker2003542-24">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup> A minority of commentators consider them to be fragments of a lost Aramaic- or Hebrew-language original.
</p><p>The <a href="/wiki/Gospel_of_Pseudo-Matthew" title="Gospel of Pseudo-Matthew">Gospel of Pseudo-Matthew</a> is a 7th-century compilation of three other texts: the <a href="/wiki/Gospel_of_James" title="Gospel of James">Gospel of James</a>, the <a href="/wiki/Flight_into_Egypt" title="Flight into Egypt">Flight into Egypt</a>, and the <a href="/wiki/Infancy_Gospel_of_Thomas" title="Infancy Gospel of Thomas">Infancy Gospel of Thomas</a>.
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Origen" title="Origen">Origen</a> said the first Gospel was written by Matthew.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEdwards200918_25-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEdwards200918-25">&#91;23&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERepschinski200014_26-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERepschinski200014-26">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup> This Gospel was composed in Hebrew near Jerusalem for Hebrew Christians and translated into Greek, but the Greek copy was lost. The Hebrew original was kept at the Library of <a href="/wiki/Early_centers_of_Christianity#Caesarea" class="mw-redirect" title="Early centers of Christianity">Caesarea</a>. The Nazarene Community transcribed a copy for Jerome<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENicholson187982_27-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENicholson187982-27">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup> which he used in his work.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESaint_Jerome200010_28-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESaint_Jerome200010-28">&#91;26&#93;</a></sup> Matthew's Gospel was called the <i>Gospel according to the Hebrews</i><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHultgrenHaggmark1996122_29-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHultgrenHaggmark1996122-29">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup> or sometimes the <i>Gospel of the Apostles</i><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENicholson187926_30-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENicholson187926-30">&#91;28&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDods1858iv_31-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDods1858iv-31">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup> and it was once believed that it was the original to the <i>Greek Matthew</i> found in the Bible.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHarrison1964152_32-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarrison1964152-32">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup> However, this has been challenged by modern biblical scholars such as <a href="/wiki/Bart_D._Ehrman" title="Bart D. Ehrman">Bart D. Ehrman</a> and <a href="/wiki/James_R._Edwards" title="James R. Edwards">James R. Edwards</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEdwards2009245_33-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEdwards2009245-33">&#91;31&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman199943_22-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman199943-22">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> See also the <a href="/wiki/Two-source_hypothesis" title="Two-source hypothesis">two-source hypothesis</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMillsWilson2003942_34-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMillsWilson2003942-34">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEpiphanius_of_Salamis1987129_35-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEpiphanius_of_Salamis1987129-35">&#91;33&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Jerome" title="Jerome">Jerome</a> relates that Matthew was supposed by the <a href="/wiki/Nazarene_(sect)" title="Nazarene (sect)">Nazarenes</a> to have composed their <i><a href="/wiki/Gospel_of_the_Hebrews" title="Gospel of the Hebrews">Gospel of the Hebrews</a></i><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESaint_Jerome200010_28-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESaint_Jerome200010-28">&#91;26&#93;</a></sup> though <a href="/wiki/Irenaeus" title="Irenaeus">Irenaeus</a> and <a href="/wiki/Epiphanius_of_Salamis" title="Epiphanius of Salamis">Epiphanius of Salamis</a> consider this simply a revised version of the <a href="/wiki/Biblical_canon" title="Biblical canon">canonical</a> Gospel. This Gospel has been partially preserved in the writings of the <a href="/wiki/Church_Fathers" title="Church Fathers">Church Fathers</a>, said to have been written by Matthew.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMillsWilson2003942_34-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMillsWilson2003942-34">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup> Epiphanius does not make his own the claim about a Gospel of the Hebrews written by Matthew, a claim that he merely attributes to the <a href="/wiki/Heresy" title="Heresy">heretical</a> <a href="/wiki/Ebionites" title="Ebionites">Ebionites</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEpiphanius_of_Salamis1987129_35-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEpiphanius_of_Salamis1987129-35">&#91;33&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:200px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Matthew_the_Apostle._Detail_of_the_mosaic_in_the_Basilica_of_San_Vitale._Ravena,_Italy.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/31/Matthew_the_Apostle._Detail_of_the_mosaic_in_the_Basilica_of_San_Vitale._Ravena%2C_Italy.jpg/198px-Matthew_the_Apostle._Detail_of_the_mosaic_in_the_Basilica_of_San_Vitale._Ravena%2C_Italy.jpg" decoding="async" width="198" height="152" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/31/Matthew_the_Apostle._Detail_of_the_mosaic_in_the_Basilica_of_San_Vitale._Ravena%2C_Italy.jpg/297px-Matthew_the_Apostle._Detail_of_the_mosaic_in_the_Basilica_of_San_Vitale._Ravena%2C_Italy.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/31/Matthew_the_Apostle._Detail_of_the_mosaic_in_the_Basilica_of_San_Vitale._Ravena%2C_Italy.jpg/396px-Matthew_the_Apostle._Detail_of_the_mosaic_in_the_Basilica_of_San_Vitale._Ravena%2C_Italy.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1586" data-file-height="1221" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Matthew_the_Apostle._Detail_of_the_mosaic_in_the_Basilica_of_San_Vitale._Ravena,_Italy.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Matthew the Apostle, detail of the mosaic in the <a href="/wiki/Basilica_of_San_Vitale" title="Basilica of San Vitale">Basilica of San Vitale</a>, <a href="/wiki/Ravenna" title="Ravenna">Ravenna</a>, 6th century</div></div></div>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Veneration">Veneration</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Matthew_the_Apostle&amp;action=edit&amp;section=5" title="Edit section: Veneration">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>Matthew is recognized as a <a href="/wiki/Saint" title="Saint">saint</a> in the <a href="/wiki/Catholic_Church" title="Catholic Church">Roman Catholic</a>, <a href="/wiki/Eastern_Orthodox_Church" title="Eastern Orthodox Church">Eastern Orthodox</a>, <a href="/wiki/Lutheran" class="mw-redirect" title="Lutheran">Lutheran</a><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEELCA200657_36-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEELCA200657-36">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup> and <a href="/wiki/Anglican" class="mw-redirect" title="Anglican">Anglican</a> churches (<i>see <a href="/wiki/St._Matthew%27s_Church_(disambiguation)" class="mw-redirect mw-disambig" title="St. Matthew&#39;s Church (disambiguation)">St. Matthew's Church</a></i>). His <a href="/wiki/Feast_day" class="mw-redirect" title="Feast day">feast day</a> is celebrated on 21 September in the West and 16 November in the East. (Those churches which follow the traditional <a href="/wiki/Julian_calendar" title="Julian calendar">Julian calendar</a> would keep the day on 29 November of the modern <a href="/wiki/Gregorian_calendar" title="Gregorian calendar">Gregorian calendar</a>, being 16 November in the Julian calendar.) He is also commemorated by the Orthodox, together with the other Apostles, on 30 June (13 July), the <a href="/wiki/Synaxis" title="Synaxis">Synaxis</a> of the Holy Apostles. His tomb is located in the crypt of <a href="/wiki/Salerno_Cathedral" title="Salerno Cathedral">Salerno Cathedral</a> in southern Italy. Matthew is <a href="/wiki/Calendar_of_saints_(Church_of_England)" title="Calendar of saints (Church of England)">remembered</a> in the <a href="/wiki/Church_of_England" title="Church of England">Church of England</a> with a <a href="/wiki/Festival_(Anglicanism)" title="Festival (Anglicanism)">Festival</a> on <a href="/wiki/September_21" title="September 21">21 September</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Calendar_37-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Calendar-37">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Like the other <a href="/wiki/Four_Evangelists" title="Four Evangelists">evangelists</a>, Matthew is often depicted in <a href="/wiki/Christian_art" title="Christian art">Christian art</a> with one of the four living creatures of Revelation 4:7.<sup id="cite_ref-38" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-38">&#91;36&#93;</a></sup> The one that accompanies him is in the form of a winged man. The three paintings of Matthew by <a href="/wiki/Caravaggio" title="Caravaggio">Caravaggio</a> in the church of <a href="/wiki/San_Luigi_dei_Francesi" title="San Luigi dei Francesi">San Luigi dei Francesi</a> in <a href="/wiki/Rome" title="Rome">Rome</a>, where he is depicted as called by Christ from his profession as a tax gatherer, are among the landmarks of <a href="/wiki/Western_art" class="mw-redirect" title="Western art">Western art</a>.
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="In_Islam">In Islam</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Matthew_the_Apostle&amp;action=edit&amp;section=6" title="Edit section: In Islam">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>The <a href="/wiki/Quran" title="Quran">Quran</a> speaks of Jesus' disciples but does not mention their names, instead referring to them as "helpers to the work of Allah".<sup id="cite_ref-39" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-39">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup> Muslim exegesis and Quran commentary, however, name them and include Matthew amongst the disciples.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENoegelWheeler200386_40-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENoegelWheeler200386-40">&#91;38&#93;</a></sup> Muslim exegesis preserves the tradition that Matthew and <a href="/wiki/Saint_Andrew" class="mw-redirect" title="Saint Andrew">Andrew</a> were the two disciples who went to Ethiopia to preach the message of <a href="/wiki/God_in_Islam" title="God in Islam">God</a>.
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="In_architecture">In architecture</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Matthew_the_Apostle&amp;action=edit&amp;section=7" title="Edit section: In architecture">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>The Basilica of Annunciation in Nazareth houses a capital that depicts Matthew the Apostle and his story regarding King Eglypus of Ethiopia and his sons. It shows how Matthew is leading them away from the demon in the far corner of the capital. The biblical story tells of Matthew converting the king and his sons to Christianity. Not only does this capital depict an act carried out by Matthew in the Bible, it also foreshadowing Matthew being a martyr. When Matthew the Apostle was murdered, he then became a martyr for the Christian religion as being killed for his faith and teachings given the demon in the corner of the capitol. The iconography of this capital helps understand the religion of the time period since it was just coming into Christendom. This shows the cross between Ethiopia and Nazareth as these are where the capitals are today.
</p>

<li id="cite_note-Eusebius-19"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-Eusebius_19-0">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Eusebius, "History of the Church" 3.39.14–17, c.&#160;325 CE, Greek text 16: "ταῦτα μὲν οὖν ἱστόρηται τῷ Παπίᾳ περὶ τοῦ Μάρκου· περὶ δὲ τοῦ Ματθαῖου ταῦτ’ εἴρηται· Ματθαῖος μὲν οὖν Ἑβραΐδι διαλέκτῳ τὰ λόγια συνετάξατο, ἡρμήνευσεν δ’ αὐτὰ ὡς ἧν δυνατὸς ἕκαστος. Various English translations published, standard reference translation by <a href="/wiki/Philip_Schaff" title="Philip Schaff">Philip Schaff</a> at <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="http://www.ccel.org/ccel/schaff/npnf201.iii.viii.xxxix.html">CCEL</a>: "[C]oncerning Matthew he [Papias] writes as follows: 'So then(963) Matthew wrote the oracles in the Hebrew language, and every one interpreted them as he was able.'(964)" Online version includes footnotes 963 and 964 by Schaff.<br /><a href="/wiki/Irenaeus" title="Irenaeus">Irenaeus of Lyons</a> (died c. 202 CE) makes a similar comment, possibly also drawing on Papias, in his <i>Against Heresies</i>, <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="http://www.ccel.org/ccel/schaff/anf01.ix.iv.ii.html">Book III, Chapter 1</a>, <i>"Matthew also issued a written Gospel among the Hebrews in their own dialect"</i></span>
</li>
</ol></div></div>
